This author is supported by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF): P 32660 Thanks: ∗∗ RICAM, Austrian Academy of Sciences, Altenbergerstrasse 69, A-4040, Linz, Austria. Email: ahcene.ghandriche@ricam.oeaw.ac.at. This author is supported by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF): P 30756-NBL Thanks: ‡ RICAM, Austrian Academy of Sciences, Altenbergerstrasse 69, A-4040, Linz, Austria. Email: mourad.sini@oeaw.ac.at. This author is partially supported by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF): P 30756-NBL and P 32660
Xinlin Cao ∗ Ahcene Ghandriche ∗∗ and Mourad Sini ‡

Abstract
. We estimate the electromagnetic fields generated by a cluster of dielectric nanoparticles embedded into a background made of a vacuum. The dielectric nanoparticles are small scaled but enjoy high contrast of their relative permittivity. Such scales/contrasts can be ensured using the Lorentz model with incident frequencies chosen appropriately close to the undamped resonance (appearing in the Lorentz model). Under certain ratio between their size and contrast, these nanoparticles generate resonances, called dielectric resonances. These resonances are characterized and computed via the spectrum of the electric Newtonian operator, stated on the support of nanoparticles, projected on the space of divergence-free fields with vanishing boundary normal components. We characterize the dominant field generated by a cluster of such dielectric-resonating nanoparticles. In this point-interaction approximation, the nanoparticles can be distributed to occupy volume-like domains or low dimensional hypersurfaces where periodicity is not required. The form of these approximations suggests that the effective electromagnetic medium, equivalent to the cluster of such nanoparticles, is a perturbation of the magnetic permeability and not the electric permittivity. The cluster can be tuned such that the equivalent permeability has positive or negative values (while the permittivity stays unchanged).



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35R30, 35C20

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

In the recent years, in the engineering community, there was a high gain of interests in studying electromagnetic wave propagating in resonating dielectric nanostructures
[
9
,
30
,
26
,
27
]
. These media are composed of the homogeneous background in which we inject nano-scaled particles. In addition to be small scaled, these particles enjoy high values of their relative index of refraction with a relatively small
Q-
factor (i.e. the ratio between the absorption and diffusion coefficients is very small). These properties allow them to enjoy interesting and useful optical properties. The smallness of the
Q-
factor allow them to be less loosy as compared with other types of nanoparticles as the plasmonic ones. As such, they are more suited to be used in imaging techniques that require remote measurements, for instance. The high contrasts of the refraction index allow them to resonate at certain scales size/contrast (or at certain ranges of incident frequencies). Such resonance’s effects are very attractive in both imaging and material sciences. They are also potentially applicable for the design of highly nonlinear material (i.e. the Kerker effect).

Our interest in this topic is to understand the interaction between the light and the nanostructure. Precisely, we want to estimate the perturbations of the used incident field due to the presence of these nanostructures.
Looking at the problem under this angle, we wish to derive the dominating terms in the expansion of the scattered field taking into account the whole structure of the composite, namely the (potentially high) number of the nanoparticles, their sizes and the high contrasts of the related indices of refraction. The derived close form of the dominating term will allow us to tune the structure, at will, so that the equivalent material will enjoy needed properties as sign changing and nonlinearity of the effective electric or magnetic susceptibility.

In this work, we focus on deriving these approximations for general shapes. This follows the lines of our previous works on point-interaction approximations (called also the Foldy-Lax approximation) for the electromagnetic fields,
[
13
,
14
,
15
]
. In
[
13
,
14
]
, we derived the close form of the dominating term in the case that the contrasts of the permittivity and the permeability of the particles are moderate. This dominating term is given in a form of a superposition of electric poles and magnetic dipoles with attached weights. These attached weights (that encode the structure of the composite) are vectors that are computable by solving an invertible algebraic system. The analysis is based on the related Lippmann-Schwinger system of equations coupling the electric and magnetic fields. This Lippmann-Schwinger system is defined through an operator that couples the electric vector Newtonian operator and the Magnetization operator. As the contrasts of the particles are moderate, both the two operators play equivalent roles. That is why the derived approximation in
[
13
,
14
]
involves contributions from the electric and magnetic polarization tensor with equivalent roles. The equivalent media generated by such moderately contrasting nanoparticles have been derived in
[
15
]
that confirms that both the electric permittivity and the magnetic permeability are perturbed. However, in the case of dielectric nanoparticles, as in our current work, the permittivity has high contrast while the permeability stays moderate (or unchanged). In this case, the effect of the vector Newtonian operator dominates the one of the Magnetization operator. In addition, under critical scales between the sizes and the contrasts, of the particles, one can excite a subfamily of the eigenvalues of the Newtonian operator. These are related to the projection of this Newtonian operator on the subspace of divergence free fields with zero normal components. This makes the analysis more involved and subtle than that in
[
13
,
14
]
. However, this is worth the efforts. Indeed, we show that even though the contrasts of the nanoparticles are due to the permittivity, the dominating field is of magnetic type.
In other words, the point-interaction approximation, due to the presence of the whole cluster of dielectric nanoparticles, suggests that the effective medium will be a perturbation of the magnetic permeability and not the electric permittivity. The justification of such effective medium, in different scenarios including the Moiré metamaterials, see
[
34
]
, will be done in a forthcoming work.

Wave propagation in resonating media is highly
attractive and, in the very recent years, we witness a rapid growth of the number of published works
in the mathematical community (in addition to the relatively large engineering literature).
These resonances occur if the particles are small-sized and enjoy high or negative contrasts of their materials. In effect, they are related to the spectrum of the volumetric operator (Newtonian potential operator) or surface operators (Neumann-Poincaré operator or related Magnetization operator) appearing in the resolvent operator modeling the wave propagation.
With such contrasts of the materials, one can excite these resonances and enhance the values of scattering coefficients (or generally the polarization tensors). Such enhancements are useful in many applications as in the effective medium theory. In this direction, let us cite the following works
[
2
,
3
,
5
,
4
,
6
]
for the acoustic propagation. As far as
the effective medium theory is concerned, few results are known for the electromagnetism
with highly contrasting or negative permittivity or permeability. We can cite
[
1
,
10
,
12
,
16
,
17
,
18
,
11
,
28
,
32
,
33
]
who assume periodicity and derive the equivalent coefficients, via the homogenization theory, for dielectric
nanoparticles. The results provided in the current work is a contribution to fill in this gap.

1.2.
Main results

Let
D
be a bounded and Lipschitz-regular domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We assume that
D
is a non-magnetic material meaning that the permeability
\mu
is constant everywhere and equals to the one of the vaccum
\mu_{0}
.
The electric permittivity is equal to the one of the vaccum outside
D
,
\epsilon_{0}
, but inside, we assume it to have different values
\epsilon_{p}
. We denote by the relative permittivity and permeability as
\epsilon_{r}:=\frac{\epsilon}{\epsilon_{0}}
and
\mu_{r}:=\frac{\mu}{\mu_{0}}
. With such notations, we have
\epsilon_{r}=1
outside
D
while
\mu_{r}=1
in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

The electromagnetic scattering of time-harmonic plane waves from the body
D
reads as follows

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llrr}Curl(E^{T})-i\,k\,H^{T}=0&\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{3}\\
\\
Curl(H^{T})+i\,k\,\varepsilon_{r}\,E^{T}=0&\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{3},\end{array}\right.

where the total field
(E^{T},H^{T})
is of the form
(E^{T}:=E^{Inc}+E^{s},H^{T}:=H^{Inc}+H^{s})
with and incident plane wave
(E^{Inc},H^{Inc})
of the form

E^{Inc}(x,\theta)=\theta^{\perp}\exp\left(i\,k\;\theta\cdot x\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad H^{Inc}(x,\theta)=\big(\theta^{\perp}\times\theta\big)\;\exp\big(i\,k\;\theta\cdot x\big),

and the scattered field
(E^{s},H^{s})
satisfies the Silver-Müller radiation conditions (SMRC) at infinity:

\sqrt{\mu_{0}\epsilon_{0}^{-1}}H^{s}(x)\times\frac{x}{|x|}-E^{s}(x)=O(\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}).

This problem is well posed in appropriate Sobolev spaces, see
[
19
,
29
]
, and we have the behaviors

(1.2)

E^{s}(x)=\frac{e^{ik|x|}}{|x|}\left(E^{\infty}(\hat{x})+O(|x|^{-1})\right),\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\rightarrow\infty,

and

H^{s}(x)=\frac{e^{ik|x|}}{|x|}\left(H^{\infty}(\hat{x})+O(|x|^{-1})\right),\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\rightarrow\infty,

where
(E^{\infty}(\hat{x}),H^{\infty}(\hat{x}))
is the corresponding electromagnetic far field pattern of (
1.1
) in the propagation direction
\hat{x}:=\frac{x}{|x|}
.

Next, we present the needed assumptions on the model (
1.1
) to derive our results.

I.
Assumptions on the cluster of particles
.
Suppose that each component
D_{m}
of
D
is of the form
D_{m}=a{B}_{m}+{z}_{m}
,
m=1,\cdots,\aleph
, which is characterized by the parameter
a>0
and the location
{z}_{m}
. We denote

a:=\max\limits_{1\leq m\leq M}\mathrm{diam}(D_{m}),\quad d:=\min\limits_{{1\leq m,j\leq M}\atop{m\neq j}}d_{mj}:=\min\limits_{{1\leq m,j\leq M}\atop{m\neq j}}\mathrm{dist}(D_{m},D_{j}).
We take

(1.3)

\aleph=O(d^{-3})\mbox{ and }d\sim a^{t}

with the nonnegative parameter
t\leq 1
. For simplicity of the exposition, we assume that the shapes of
B_{m}
’s are the same and we denote
B:=B_{m}
. The domain
B
is a bounded Lipschitz domain that contains the origin.

II.
Assumption on the shape of
B
.
Define the vector Newtonian operator
N^{0}
. Denote
e_{n}^{(1)}
as the corresponding eigenfunctions over the subspace
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
. Since
\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)\equiv Curl\left(\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl\right)\cap\mathbb{H}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)\right),

see for instance
[
8
]
, then we have

(1.4)

e_{n_{0}}^{(1)}=Curl(\phi_{n_{0}})\,\,\mbox{ with }\,\,\nu\times\phi_{n_{0}}=0\,\,\text{and}\,\,\mathrm{div}\,(\phi_{n_{0}})=0.

We assume that, for a certain
n_{0}
,

\int_{B}\phi_{n_{0}}(y)\,dy\neq 0.

III.
Assumptions on the permittivity and permeability of each particle.
In order to investigate the electromagnetic scattering of dielectric nanoparticles with high contrast electric permittivity parameter, we assume that for a constant
\varsigma
,

(1.5)

\eta:=\epsilon_{r}-1=\varsigma\;a^{-2},\;\mbox{ with }\;\;\varsigma<1\;\;\penalty\ \penalty\ a\ll 1,

and the magnetic permeability
\mu_{r}
to be moderate, namely
\mu_{r}=1
.

IV.
Assumption on the used incident frequency
k
.
There exists a positive constant
c_{0}
such that

(1.6)

1\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\,=\,\pm\;c_{0}\;a^{h},\;\penalty\ \penalty\ a\ll 1,

where
\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}
is the eigenvalue corresponding to
e_{n_{0}}^{(1)}
.

Remark 1.1
.

The conditions (
1.5
) and (
1.6
) can be derived from the Lorentz model by choosing appropriate incident frequency
k
. Indeed, we recall the Lorentz model for the relative permittivity

\epsilon_{r}=1-\dfrac{k_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}}{k_{0}^{2}-k^{2}-ik\gamma}

where
k_{\mathrm{p}}
is the plasmonic resonance,
k_{0}
is the undamped resonance and
\gamma
is the damping frequency. We also recall the eigenvalues
\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}
of the Newtonian operator stated on
B
, and the contrast parameter
\eta=\epsilon_{r}-1
.
If the frequency of the incident wave
k
is chosen to be real and
k^{2}
close to the undamped resonance frequency
k^{2}_{0}
with

k^{2}-k_{0}^{2}=\frac{k_{p}^{2}\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\,k_{0}^{2}}{\left(1\mp\bm{{\mathrm{Re\,}}}(c_{0})\,a^{h}\right)\pm\dfrac{\bm{{\mathrm{Im\,}}}^{2}(c_{0})\,a^{h}}{\left(1\mp\bm{{\mathrm{Re\,}}}(c_{0})\,a^{h}\right)}-k_{p}^{2}\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}}=k_{p}^{2}\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\,k_{0}^{2}\,\left[1+\mathcal{O}(a^{h})\right]

and

\gamma k=\pm\frac{\bm{{\mathrm{Im\,}}}(c_{0})\,a^{2}\,(k^{2}-k^{2}_{0})}{\left(1\mp\bm{{\mathrm{Re\,}}}(c_{0})\,a^{h}\right)}=\pm\,\bm{{\mathrm{Im\,}}}(c_{0})\,a^{4}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\,k_{0}^{2}\,k_{p}^{2}\left[1+\mathcal{O}(a^{h})\right],

then we obtain
\bm{{\mathrm{Re\,}}}\Big(\eta\Big)=a^{-2}\Big(\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\,k_{0}^{2}\Big)^{-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{2}\right)
and
\bm{{\mathrm{Im\,}}}\Big(\eta\Big)=\mp\,\bm{{\mathrm{Im\,}}}\Big(c_{0}\Big)\,\Big(\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\,k_{0}^{2}\Big)^{-1}+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{4}\right)
. With these choices, we see that
\frac{k^{2}}{k_{0}^{2}}\sim 1
. In addition, we derive the needed relation
1-k^{2}\eta a^{2}\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}=\pm\;c_{0}\;a^{h},\;\penalty\ \penalty\ a\ll 1
with a given value of
c_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
. In practice, an additional condition on the sign of
\bm{{\mathrm{Im\,}}}(c_{0})
might be needed to ensure the non-negativity of the damping, i.e.
\bm{{\mathrm{Im\,}}}(\epsilon_{r})\geq 0
. In particular, if
\bm{{\mathrm{Im\,}}}(c_{0})=0
then
\gamma=0
and then we get the Drude’s model.

Remark 1.2
.

From the previous remark we deduce that
\varsigma
, given in
(\ref{contrast-epsilon})
,
can be approximated by

\varsigma=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\;k_{0}^{2}\;\left(1\pm i\,\bm{{\mathrm{Im\,}}}\Big(c_{0}\Big)\,a^{2}\right)\,\left(1+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{h}\right)\right)}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\;k_{0}^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{h}\right),

and the condition
\bm{{\mathrm{Re\,}}}(\varsigma)<1
will be fulfilled as long as
1<\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\;k_{0}^{2}
with
a\ll 1
. If
\bm{{\mathrm{Im\,}}}(c_{0})=0
then
\varsigma
is real valued.

Based on the above conditions, we are now in a position to state our main result.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let the conditions (I, II, III, IV) on the electromagnetic scattering problem (
1.1
), by the multiple particles,
D_{1},\cdots,D_{\aleph}
, be satisfied. For
t
and
h
in
[0,1]
such that

(1.7)

3-3t-h\geq 0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ and }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{9}{11}<h<1,

the far field of the scattered wave admits the following expansion

(1.8)

E^{\infty}(\hat{x})=-\,i\,k\,\eta\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times Q_{m}+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{h}{3}}\right),

where

\left(Q_{m}\right)_{m=1,\cdots,\aleph}
is the vector solution to the algebraic system

(1.9)

Q_{m}-\eta\,k^{2}\,a^{5-h}\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq m}^{\aleph}{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{m},z_{j})\cdot{Q}_{j}=i\,k\,a^{5-h}\;{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{m}),

where
\Upsilon_{k}
is the dyadic Green’s function given by (
2.3
) and
{\bf{P}}_{0}
is the polarization matrix defined by

(1.10)

{\bf{P}}_{0}:=\sum_{m}\int_{B}\phi_{n_{0},m}(y)\,dy\otimes\int_{B}\phi_{n_{0},m}(y)\,dy,

where
\phi_{n_{0},m}
fulfills

e_{n_{0},m}^{(1)}=Curl\left(\phi_{n_{0},m}\right),\,\,\mathrm{div}\,\left(\phi_{n_{0},m}\right)=0,\,\,\nu\times\phi_{n_{0},m}=0\,\,\text{and}\,\,N\left(e_{n_{0},m}^{(1)}\right)=\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\,e_{n_{0},m}^{(1)}.

In particular, (
1.9
) is invertible under the condition that

\frac{k^{2}|\eta|a^{5}}{d^{3}\left|1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\right|}\left\lVert{\bf{P}}_{0}\right\rVert<1.

We observe from (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) that if the number of the particles behaves like
\aleph\sim d^{-3}(\sim a^{-3t})
, with
3-3t-h=0
, and the particles are distributed in a bounded domain
\Omega_{eff}
, then, at least formally, the electric field is equivalent to the one generated by an effective medium given by
(\epsilon_{eff}:=\epsilon_{0},\mu_{eff}:=\mu_{0}+\mu_{pert}\chi_{\Omega})
where
\mu_{pert}
is related to the cluster of particles and it can be positive or negative (depending on the chosen sign in (
1.6
)). In particular, we see that even though the dielectric nanoparticles are merely generated by the contrasts of their permittivity (and not their permeability), the effective medium is a perturbation of the permeability and not the permittivity.

Before closing this introduction, let us mention that quite recently, after closing the content of the current work, we got aware of the work
[
7
]
, where the authors derived similar approximation formulas in the case of a single dielectric nanoparticle.
We note that the error term in (
1.8
), of the order
\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{h}{3}}\right)
, takes into account the number of particles of the order
\aleph=O(a^{-3t})
with
t
satisfying (
1.7
). For the case of a single particle, this error term is smaller and it is dominated by the first term in (
1.8
), with
\aleph=1
. This can be seen at the end of Section
4
with more details.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce some preliminaries including the Lippmann-Schwinger system of equations for the solution to (
1.1
) and the needed decomposition of the vector
\mathbb{L}^{2}
space via the subspaces
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
,
\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)
and
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
. Then we present some needed a-priori estimates of the projections of the solution on these three subspaces, respectively, which will be proved later in Section
5
. In Section
3
, we first investigate the general expressions of the linear algebraic system and the corresponding invertibility conditions. Then we formulate the precise form of the linear algebraic systems associated to the projection of the total wave onto the two subspaces
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
and
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
, respectively. Detailed proofs will be showed later in Section
6
. Section
4
is devoted to show the rigorous proof of our main theorem, in particular the Foldy-Lax approximation of the far-field. Finally, in Section
5
and Section
6
, we give the detailed proofs of the a-prior estimates introduced in Section
2
and the construction of the linear algebraic systems in Section
3
, respectively.

2.
Some preliminary knowledge and a-prior estimates.

2.1.
Decomposition of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
.

The following direct sum provide a useful decomposition of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
(see
[
20
]
, page 314)

(2.1)

\mathbb{L}^{2}=\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)\overset{\perp}{\oplus}\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right)\overset{\perp}{\oplus}\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{E\in\left(\mathbb{L}^{2}(D)\right)^{3},\,\mathrm{div}\,E=0,\,\nu\cdot E=0\,\;\text{on}\;\,\partial D\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{E\in\left(\mathbb{L}^{2}(D)\right)^{3},\,Curl\,E=0,\,\nu\times E=0\,\;\text{on}\;\,\partial D\right\},

and

\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic:=\left\{E:\;E=\nabla\psi,\,\psi\in\mathbb{H}^{1}(D),\,\Delta\psi=0\right\}.

From the decomposition (
2.1
),
we define
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}},\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}
and
\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}
to be the natural projectors as follows

(2.2)

\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}:=\mathbb{L}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right),\;\;\;\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}:=\mathbb{L}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right)\;\;\text{and}\;\;\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}:=\mathbb{L}^{2}\longrightarrow\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic.

2.2.
Lippmann-Schwinger integral formulation of the solutions.

Recall the Green’s function for the Helmholtz operator

\Phi_{k}(x,y)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{e^{ik|x-y|}}{|x-y|},\quad x\neq y,

and the corresponding dyadic Green’s function

(2.3)

\Upsilon_{k}(x,y):=\underset{x}{Hess}\Phi_{k}(x,y)+k^{2}\Phi_{k}(x,y){I},\quad x\neq y.

For any vector function
F
, define the Newtonian potential operator
N^{k}
and the Magnetization operator
\nabla M^{k}
as follows:

(2.4)

N^{k}(F)(x):=\int_{D}\Phi_{k}(x,y)F(y)\,dy\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla M^{k}(F)(x):=\underset{x}{\nabla}\int_{D}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,y)\cdot F(y)\,dy.

The solution to (
1.1
) of the integral form can be formulated as the following proposition.

Proposition 2.1
.

The solution to the electromagnetic scattering problem (
1.1
) satisfies

(2.5)

E^{T}(x)-\eta\,\int_{D}\Upsilon_{k}(x,y)\cdot E^{T}(y)\,dy=E^{Inc}(x),\quad x\in D,

where
\eta:=\epsilon_{r}-1
is the contrast of the electric permittivity, and
\Upsilon_{k}(x,y)
is the dyadic Green’s function defined by (
2.3
). Equivalently, we have

(2.6)

E^{T}(x)+\eta\,\nabla M^{k}(E^{T})(x)-k^{2}\,\eta\,N^{k}(E^{T})(x)=E^{Inc}(x),\quad x\in D,

by virtue of the Newtonian operator
N^{k}
and the Magnetization operator
\nabla M^{k}
given by (
2.4
).

The proposition can be proved by utilizing the Stratton-Chu formula directly, see
[
19
, Theorem 6.1]
for more detailed discussions.

In addition, in (
2.3
), we have

(2.7)

\displaystyle\underset{x}{Hess}\Phi_{k}(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{x}{Hess}\Phi_{0}(x,y)+\underset{x}{Hess}(\Phi_{k}-\Phi_{0})(x,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{x}{Hess}\Phi_{0}(x,y)+\frac{1}{4\pi}\;\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(ik)^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\;\underset{x}{Hess}\left(\left\|x-y\right\|^{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underset{x}{Hess}\Phi_{0}(x,y)-\frac{k^{2}}{2}\,\Phi_{0}(x,y)\,I_{3}-\frac{ik^{3}}{24\pi}\,I_{3}+\frac{k^{2}}{2}\,\Phi_{0}(x,y)\,\frac{A(x,y)}{\|x-y\|^{2}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\;\sum_{n\geq 3}\frac{(ik)^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\;\underset{x}{Hess}\left(\left\|x-y\right\|^{n}\right),

where
A
is the matrix given by
A(x,y):=\left(x-y\right)\otimes\left(x-y\right).

Then, we can write the Magnetization operator
\nabla M^{k}
with the help of (
2.7
) as

(2.8)

\displaystyle\nabla M^{k}(F)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla M^{0}(F)(x)+\frac{k^{2}}{2}\,N^{0}(F)(x)+\frac{ik^{3}}{12\pi}\int_{D}F(y)dy-\frac{k^{2}}{2}\int_{D}\Phi_{0}(x,y)\frac{A(x,y)\cdot F(y)}{\|x-y\|^{2}}dy

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\;\sum_{n\geq 3}\frac{(ik)^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\;\int_{D}\;\underset{x}{Hess}\left(\left\|x-y\right\|^{n}\right)\cdot F(y)\,dy.

Similarly, we write an analogous formula for the Newtonian potential operator
N^{k}
as

(2.9)

N^{k}(F)(x)=N^{0}(F)(x)+\frac{ik}{4\pi}\int_{D}F(y)dy+\frac{1}{4\pi}\;\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(ik)^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\;\int_{D}\;\left\|x-y\right\|^{n}F(y)\,dy.

For shortness reason, we use the notation
\nabla M
instead of
\nabla M^{0}
and
N
instead of
N^{0}
in the subsequent analyses.

Thanks to Green’s formulas, and recalling the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-space decomposition (
2.1
), we have

(2.10)

\forall\;E\in\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right),\;\,\nabla M(E)=0\,\quad\text{and}\quad\forall\;E\in\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right),\;\,\nabla M(E)=E,

and other nice properties for the Magnetization operator, such as self-adjointness, positivity, spectrum, boundedness (
\left\|\nabla M\right\|=1
) , invariance of
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
, etc., can be found in
[
31
]
and
[
22
]
.

2.3.
A-prior Estimates.

Based on the decomposition ((
2.1
)), we present here some necessary a-prior estimates derived from the Lippmann-Schwinger equation (
2.6
), which play an important role in the proof of our main results. The proof of the propositions and lemmas in this subsection shall be given later in Section
5
.

Following the notations in (
2.2
), we denote

\ast

\left(\lambda^{(1)}_{n};e^{(1)}_{n}\right)
as the eigensystem of the operator
N
projected on the subspace
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
,

\ast

\left(\lambda^{(2)}_{n};e^{(2)}_{n}\right)
as the eigensystem of the operator
N
projected on the subspace
\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)
,

\ast

\left(\lambda^{(3)}_{n};e^{(3)}_{n}\right)
as the eigensystem of the operator
\nabla M
on the subspace
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
.

For the existence and the construction of
\left(\lambda^{(j)}_{n};e^{(j)}_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}},j=1,2,3
, we refer to Section 5 of
[
23
]
.

Now, suppose
E^{T}
solves (
1.1
) with the integral formulation (
2.6
). Then the projection of
E^{T}
onto three subspaces
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
,
\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)
and
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
can be respectively represented as
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T})
,
\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T})
and
\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T})
.
Then we have the following estimates.

2.3.1.
Estimation for one particle.

Proposition 2.2
.

Under Assumptions (I, II, III, IV), consider the electromagnetic scattering problem (
1.1
) with only one distributed particle. Let
k
fulfill

(2.11)

k^{2}:=\frac{1\mp c_{0}\,a^{h}}{\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}}\,\sim 1,

where
\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}
is an eigenvalue of the Newtonian potential operator in the subspace
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
. Then for
h<2
, there holds

(2.12)

\lVert\tilde{E}^{T}\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}=\mathcal{O}(a^{1-h}),

and in particular,

(2.13)

\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right)=0,

where
\tilde{E}^{T}
is the total wave of
E^{T}
after scaling from
D
to
B
.

The detailed proof of Proposition
2.2
can be seen in Subsection
5.1
. Based on the above a-priori estimate for one particle, we also investigate the estimation of the solution in the presence of multiple particles.

2.3.2.
Estimation for several particles.

Proposition 2.3
.

Under Assumptions (I, II, III, IV), consider the electromagnetic scattering problem (
1.1
) for multiple particles
D_{j}
,
j=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
. Then for
t
and
h
satisfying

(2.14)

3-3t-h\geq 0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mbox{ and }\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{9}{11}<h<1,

there holds the estimation

(2.15)

\max_{j}\left\lVert\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{j}^{T}\right)\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\lesssim a^{2-2h}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\max_{j}\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{j}^{T}\right)\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\lesssim a^{3+h}.

From (
2.15
), we expect that for
j=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
,
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{T}_{j}\right)
dominates.
To clear the structure of the paper, we also postpone the proof of Proposition
2.3
to Subsection
5.2
.

2.3.3.
Estimation for the scattering coefficient

Recall the solution to the electromagnetic scattering problem with the form (
2.6
). Let
W
be the solution to

(2.16)

\left(I+\eta\,\nabla M^{-k}-k^{2}\,\eta\,N^{-k}\right)\left(W\right)(x)=\mathcal{P}(x,z),\quad x\in D,

where
\nabla M^{-k}
and
N^{-k}
are the adjoint operators to
\nabla M^{k}
and
N^{k}
introduced in (
2.4
), respectively, and
\mathcal{P}(x,z)
is the matrix form of the vector
(x-z)
, which can be expressed by

\mathcal{P}(x,z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(x-z)_{1}I_{3}\\
(x-z)_{2}I_{3}\\
(x-z)_{3}I_{3}\end{array}\right),

with
(x-z)_{i}
,
i=1,2,3
, being the corresponding components. Define the scattering coefficient as

(2.17)

\mathcal{C}:=\int_{D}W(x)\,dx=\int_{D}\left(I+\eta\nabla M^{-k}-k^{2}\,\eta\,N^{-k}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathcal{P}(\cdot,z)\right)(x)\,dx.

Then there holds the following estimates of
\mathcal{C}
.

Proposition 2.4
.

The scattering coefficient
\mathcal{C}
defined by (
2.17
) satisfies

(2.18)

\mathcal{C}\sim a^{4-h}\,\sum_{n}\,\frac{1}{\left(1+\eta\,\lambda_{n}^{(3)}\right)}\,\langle\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(N\left(e_{n}^{(3)}\right)\right);e^{(1)}_{n_{0}}\rangle\langle\mathcal{P}(\cdot,0);e^{(1)}_{n_{0}}\rangle\otimes\int_{B}e^{(3)}_{n}(x)\,dx=\mathcal{O}\left(a^{6-h}\right),

where
\lambda_{n}^{(3)}
,
e_{n_{0}}^{(1)}
and
e_{n}^{(3)}
are of the eigensystem introduced in Subsection
2.3
. Moreover, aftering scaling from
D
to
B
, the solution
W
to (
2.16
) satisfies

(2.19)

\left\lVert\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{W}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}=\mathcal{O}(a^{1-h}),\quad\left\lVert\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{W}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}=\mathcal{O}(a^{3}),\quad\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{W}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}=\mathcal{O}(a^{3}).

We refer to Subsection
5.3
for the proof of Proposition
2.4
.

3.
Linear algebraic system and correponding invertibility discussions.

In this section, we first present a linear algebraic system of general form and study the corresponding invertibility condition. Then, by projecting the solution
E
onto the two subspaces
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
and
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
, we give the precise formulation of the linear algebraic systerms with respect to
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}\right)
and
\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}\right)
, respectively. The estimates for the significant polarization tensors appearing in the linear algebraic systems related to those two subspaces are also investigated. These expressions and estimations are essentially related to our main result of the Foldy-Lax approximation to the far-field.

3.1.
General linear algebraic system and corresponding invertibility condition.

Recall that
D_{j}
,
j=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
, are the cluster of particles introduced in Assumption (I, II, III, IV).
Denote by
[P_{D_{j}}]
,
j=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
, the polarization tensors, which carry the information of the geometry of the scatterer and the material parameters for the electromagnetic scattering problem (
1.1
).

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
J_{j}
,
j=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
, be a sequence of vectors. The linear algebraic system

(3.1)

Q_{j_{0}}-\eta\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq j_{0}}^{\aleph}[P_{D_{j_{0}}}]\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{j_{0}},z_{j})\cdot Q_{j}=J_{j_{0}},\quad j_{0}=1,2,\cdots,\aleph,

is invertible under the condition that

(3.2)

|\eta|\,\max_{j=1,...,\aleph}\left\lVert[P_{D_{j}}]\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)}\,d^{-3}\,<1.

We refer to Section
6
for the detailed proof of this proposition. On the basis of Proposition
3.1
, next, we present two concrete forms of the linear algebraic systems with respect to
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T}_{j})
and
\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T}_{j})
,
j=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
, which shall play an essential role in the study of our main approximation result for the far-field.

3.2.
Construction of the linear algebraic system for
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T})
.

Recall that

(3.3)

\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)=Curl(\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl)\cap\mathbb{H}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)).

For
j=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
,
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T}_{j})
denotes the projection of the total wave
E^{T}
onto the subspace
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
with respect to the particle
D_{j}
. Then from (
3.3
), we can write

(3.4)

\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T}_{j})=Curl(F_{j})\quad\mbox{with}\quad\nu\times F_{j}=0,\ \mathrm{div}\,(F_{j})=0.

Setting

(3.5)

Q_{j}:=\int_{D_{j}}F_{j}(y)\,dy,

we can construct the following precise form of the linear algebraic system with respect to
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T}_{j})
, with the help of the Lippmann-Schwinger equation (
2.6
).

Proposition 3.2
.

Under Assumption ( I, II, III, IV), there holds the following linear algebraic systerm associated with the electromagnetic scattering problem (
1.1
) related to
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T}_{j_{0}})
,
j_{0}=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
, as

(3.6)

Q_{j_{0}}-\eta\,k^{2}\,a^{5-h}\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq j_{0}}^{\aleph}{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{j_{0}},z_{j})\cdot Q_{j}\,=i\,k\,a^{5-h}\,{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{j_{0}})+Error,

where
{\bf{P}}_{0}
is defined by
(\ref{defP0})
and

(3.7)

Error:=\mathcal{O}\left(a^{9-2h}d^{-4}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{6-h}\right).

Moreover, the linear algebraic systerm (
3.6
) is invertible if

(3.8)

\frac{k^{2}\,|\eta|a^{5}}{d^{3}\,\left|1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\right|}\left\lVert{\bf{P}}_{0}\right\rVert\,\,<1.

It is easy to see from (
3.1
) and (
3.6
) that in Proposition
3.2
, we take

[P_{D_{j_{0}}}]:=k^{2}\,a^{5-h}\,{\bf{P}}_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad J_{j_{0}}:=i\,k\,a^{5-h}\,{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{j_{0}})+Error.

The detailed discussions on the construction of Proposition
3.2
can be found in Section 6. In a similar manner, in
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
, we also have the following form of the linear algebraic systerm with respect to
\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T}_{j})
,
j=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
.

3.3.
Construction of the linear algebraic system for
\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T})
.

We set here

Q_{j}:=\int_{D_{j}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{j}\right)(y)\,dy.

Proposition 3.3
.

Under Assumption ( I, II, III, IV), there holds the following linear algebraic system associated with the electromagnetic problem (
1.1
) with respect to
\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E^{T}_{j_{0}})
,
j_{0}=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
, as

(3.9)

\displaystyle Q_{j_{0}}-\eta\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq j_{0}}^{\aleph}{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{j_{0}},z_{j})\cdot Q_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot E^{Inc}_{j_{0}}(z_{j_{0}})+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(6,8-h-3t)}\right),

where
{\bf{P}}_{1}
is the polarization matrix defined by

(3.10)

{\bf{P}}_{1}:=a^{3}\,\sum_{n}\frac{1}{1+\eta\,\lambda_{n}^{(3)}}\int_{B}e_{n}^{(3)}(y)\,dy\otimes\int_{B}e_{n}^{(3)}(y)\,dy=a^{3}\,\sum_{n}\frac{1}{1+\eta\,\lambda_{n}^{(3)}}\,\sum_{m}\int_{B}e_{n,m}^{(3)}(y)\,dy\otimes\int_{B}e_{n,m}^{(3)}(y)\,dy,

where
e_{n,m}^{(3)}
is the eigenvalue such that
\nabla M\left(e_{n,m}^{(3)}\right)=\lambda_{n}^{(3)}\,e_{n,m}^{(3)}
. Moreover, the linear algebraic systerm (
3.9
) is invertible if

(3.11)

\frac{|\eta|\,a^{3}}{d^{3}}\,\left\lVert\sum_{n}\frac{1}{1+\eta\,\lambda_{n}^{(3)}}\int_{B}e_{n}^{(3)}(y)\,dy\otimes\int_{B}e_{n}^{(3)}(y)\,dy\right\rVert<1.

Combining with (
3.1
) and (
3.9
), we notice that in Proposition
3.3
, we take
[P_{D_{j_{0}}}]:={\bf{P}}_{1}
and

J_{j_{0}}:={\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot E^{Inc}_{j_{0}}(z_{j_{0}})+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(6,8-h-3t)}\right).

The proof of Proposition
3.3
can be seen in Section
6
.

Remark 3.4
.

Indeed, the linear algebraic systems
(\ref{la-1})
and
(\ref{la-2})
can be seen as two particular cases of the following general form

(3.12)

\left[\mathbf{I}+\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\bm{B_{11}}&\bm{B_{12}}\\
\hline\cr\bm{B_{21}}&\bm{B_{22}}\end{array}\right)\right]\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\int_{D_{1}}F_{1}(y)dy\\
\vdots\\
\int_{D_{\aleph}}F_{\aleph}(y)dy\\
\hline\cr\int_{D_{1}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{1}\right)(y)\,dy\\
\vdots\\
\int_{D_{\aleph}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{\aleph}\right)(y)\,dy\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}i\,k\,a^{5-h}\,{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
\vdots\\
i\,k\,a^{5-h}\,{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{\aleph})\\
\hline\cr{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
\vdots\\
{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{\aleph})\end{pmatrix}+Error,

where

\displaystyle\bm{B_{11}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\eta\,k^{2}\,a^{5-h}\,\begin{pmatrix}0&{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})&\cdots&{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{1},z_{\aleph})\\
{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})&0&\cdots&{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{2},z_{\aleph})\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{\aleph},z_{1})&{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{\aleph},z_{2})&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\bm{B_{22}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-\eta\,\begin{pmatrix}0&{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})&\cdots&{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{1},z_{\aleph})\\
{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})&0&\cdots&{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{2},z_{\aleph})\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{\aleph},z_{1})&{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{\aleph},z_{2})&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}

and
\mathbf{I}
is the identity matrix. The linear algebraic system
(\ref{GAS})
is the one corresponding to the Lippmann-Schwinger equation given by:

(3.13)

E^{T}(x)+\eta\,\nabla M^{k}(E^{T})(x)-k^{2}\,\eta\,N^{k}(E^{T})(x)=E^{Inc}(x),\quad x\in D,

seeing
(\ref{LS eq2})
. Since there are two operators presented in (
3.13
), namely the Magnetization operator and the Newtonian operator, we distinguish the following two cases in terms of the choice of the incident frequencies to enhance the scattered electromagnetic fields.

(1)

On the case when the incident frequencies are chosen to generate plasmonic resonances
. In this case, we can prove that the field corresponding to the Magnetization operator
\nabla M(\cdot)
is the dominating one in equation
(\ref{LSERemark})
, and consequently, the matrix block
\bm{B_{22}}
dominates the other blocks. Hence,
(\ref{GAS})
will be reduced to
(\ref{la-2})
. The analysis of this case for a single plasmonic nano-particle was the object of
[
23
]
.

(2)

On the case when the incident frequencies are chosen to generate dielectric resonances
. In this case, we can prove that the field generated by the Newtonian operator
N(\cdot)
is the dominating one in equation
(\ref{LSERemark})
, and consequently, the matrix block
\bm{B_{11}}
dominates the other blocks. Hence,
(\ref{GAS})
will be reduced to
(\ref{la-1})
. The goal of the current work is to analyze this case in details.

Furthermore, regardless of the chosen resonances, we can prove the smallness of the two matrices
\bm{B_{12}}
and
\bm{B_{21}}
, seeing the proof of Proposition
3.2
and Proposition
3.3
.

4.
Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

With all the necessary propositions presented in the previous sections, we give the proof of our main result in this section as follows.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

We have

\displaystyle E_{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\;\int_{D}e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot y}\;E^{T}(y)\;dy,\quad D=\overset{\aleph}{\underset{m=1}{\cup}}D_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\;\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\int_{D_{m}}e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot y}\;E_{m}^{T}(y)\;dy,

where
E_{m}^{T}(\cdot):={E^{T}(\cdot)_{|}}_{D_{m}}
and
D_{m}=z_{m}+a\,B
. By developing near the center, we obtain

\displaystyle E_{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\;\ \sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\;\int_{D_{m}}\left[e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}+i\,k\,\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,(y-z_{m})\cdot\hat{x}\right]\;E^{T}_{m}(y)\;dy

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\;\ \sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\;\int_{D_{m}}\frac{k^{2}}{2}\left((y-z_{m})\cdot\hat{x}\right)^{2}\,\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot\left(z_{m}+t(y-z_{m})\right)}\,dt\;E^{T}_{m}(y)\;dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\;\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\left[\int_{D_{m}}E^{T}_{m}(y)\,dy+i\,k\,\int_{D_{m}}\hat{x}\cdot(y-z_{m})\;E^{T}_{m}(y)\,dy\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{3}{2}}\,\aleph\,\max_{m}\left\|E_{m}^{T}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{m})}\right).

Based on Proposition
2.2
, we split
E^{T}_{m}
as
E^{T}_{m}:=\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)+\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)
and we plug it into the previous equation to obtain

\displaystyle E_{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\left[\int_{D_{m}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy+i\,k\,\int_{D_{m}}\hat{x}\cdot(y-z_{m})\;\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\left[i\,k\,\int_{D_{m}}\hat{x}\cdot(y-z_{m})\;\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{3}{2}}\,\aleph\,\max_{m}\left\|E^{T}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{m})}\right).

Since from (
2.15
) of Proposition
2.3
, we have that

\max_{m}\left\|E^{T}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{m})}=a^{\frac{3}{2}}\max_{m}\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\lesssim a^{\frac{3}{2}}\cdot a^{1-h}=\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{5}{2}-h}\right),

in which
\left\|E^{T}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{m})}
is dominated by
\left\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{m})}
. Combining with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the scaling property, we can derive

(4.1)

\displaystyle E_{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\left[\int_{D_{m}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy+i\,k\,\int_{D_{m}}\hat{x}\cdot(y-z_{m})\;\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(a^{2}\,\aleph\,\max_{m}\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{T}_{m}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(a\right),

where we use the fact that
\aleph=O(d^{-3})\sim a^{-3t}
with
t\leq 1-\frac{h}{3}
, in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which is imposed by the invertibility condition of the linear algebraic systems. In particular, using Proposition
2.3
again, for
\frac{9}{11}<h<1
, we can further simplify (
4.1
) as

E_{\infty}(\hat{x})=\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\left[\int_{D_{m}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy+i\,k\,\int_{D_{m}}\hat{x}\cdot(y-z_{m})\;\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(a\right).

By (
3.4
), we know that there holds

(4.2)

\displaystyle E_{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\int_{D_{m}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\eta\,i\,k\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\hat{x}\times\int_{D_{m}}F_{m}(y)\,dy+\mathcal{O}\left(a\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\int_{D_{m}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy-\eta\,i\,k\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\hat{x}\times\int_{D_{m}}F_{m}(y)\,dy+\mathcal{O}\left(a\right),

where the vector
\left(\int_{D_{m}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy\right)_{m=1,\cdots,\aleph}
is the solution to (
3.9
). Indeed, in (
3.9
), by denoting

(4.3)

\mathcal{S}_{ource,j_{0}}:={\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot E^{Inc,j_{0}}(z_{j_{0}}),

it yields,

(4.4)

\int_{D_{j_{0}}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{j_{0}}\right)(x)\,dx-\eta\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq j_{0}}^{\aleph}{\bf{P}}_{1}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{j_{0}},z_{j})\cdot\int_{D_{j}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{j}\right)(y)\,dy=\mathcal{S}_{ource,j_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(a^{\min(6,8-h-3t)}).

Under the invertibility condition (
3.11
), in Proposition
3.3
, now we can give the estimation for the first term in (
4.2
) on the basis of (
4.4
) as follows.

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\int_{D_{m}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E^{T}_{m}\right)(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\left|L_{1}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\eta\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\mathcal{S}_{ource,m}\right|\lesssim|\eta|\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}|\mathcal{S}_{ource,m}|,

using the definition of
\mathcal{S}_{ource,m}
in
(\ref{Sourceterm})
, we obtain that

(4.5)

\left|L_{1}\right|\lesssim\left|\eta\right|\,\left|{\bf{P}}_{1}\right|\,\aleph\overset{(\ref{DefP1S})}{=}\mathcal{O}\left(\left|\eta\right|\,a^{5}\,\aleph\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(a^{h}\right),

where we use the fact that
d\sim a^{t}
,
t\leq 1-\frac{h}{3}
and
\aleph=\mathcal{O}\left(d^{-3}\right)
, seeing
(\ref{cluster})
.

Therefore, equation (
4.2
) becomes,

E_{\infty}(\hat{x})=-i\,k\,\eta\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\left[\hat{x}\times\int_{D_{m}}F_{m}(y)\,dy\right]+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{h}\right)=-i\,k\,\eta\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times Q_{m}+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{h}\right),

following the notation (
3.5
) presented in Proposition
3.2
,
where the vector
\left(Q_{m}\right)_{m=1,\cdots,\aleph}
is the solution to the linear algebraic system (
3.6
).

Consider the linear algebraic system

(4.6)

\hat{Q}_{m}-\eta\,k^{2}\,a^{5-h}\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq m}^{\aleph}{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{m},z_{j})\cdot\hat{Q}_{j}=i\,k\,a^{5-h}\,{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\hat{H}^{Inc}(z_{m}).

Substracting (
4.6
) from (
3.6
), it is clear that there holds

(4.7)

(Q_{m}-\hat{Q}_{m})-\eta\,k^{2}\,a^{5-h}\,\sum_{j=1\atop j\neq m}^{\aleph}{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{m},z_{j})\cdot(Q_{j}-\hat{Q}_{j})=i\,k\,a^{5-h}\,{\bf{P}}_{0}\cdot(H^{Inc}(z_{m})-\hat{H}^{Inc}(z_{m}))+Error,

where
\hat{H}^{Inc}(z_{m})=-\,i\,k^{-1}\,a^{h-5}\,{\bf{P}}_{0}^{-1}\cdot Error
and
Error
possesses the expression (
3.7
).

Combining with the right hand side of (
3.6
) as well as the invertibility condition (
3.8
), we can know by direct verification that for any
m=1,2,\cdots,\aleph
,
\hat{Q}_{m}
fulfills

\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}|\hat{Q}_{m}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{k\,a^{5-h}\left\lVert{\bf{P}}_{0}\right\rVert}{1-\dfrac{k^{2}\,\left|\eta\right|a^{5}}{d^{3}|1-k^{2}\eta a^{2}\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}|}\left\lVert{\bf{P}}_{0}\right\rVert}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\aleph}|\hat{H}^{Inc}(z_{m})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

which indicates that in (
4.7
), we have

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}|Q_{m}-\hat{Q}_{m}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{k\,a^{5-h}\,\left\lVert{\bf{P}}_{0}\right\rVert}{1-\dfrac{k^{2}\,|\eta|\,a^{5}}{d^{3}\left|1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\right|}\left\lVert{\bf{P}}_{0}\right\rVert}\,\left(a^{2h-10}\,k^{-2}\,\left\lVert{\bf{P}}_{0}^{-1}\right\rVert^{2}\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}|Error|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\lVert{\bf{P}}_{0}\right\rVert\,\left\lVert{\bf{P}}_{0}^{-1}\right\rVert}{1-\dfrac{k^{2}\,|\eta|\,a^{5}}{d^{3}\,\left|1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\right|}\left\lVert{\bf{P}}_{0}\right\rVert}\,\aleph^{\frac{1}{2}}\,|Error|.

Combining with the a-priori estimates (
2.15
) given in Proposition
2.3
, by direct calculations, we can deduce that

\displaystyle E_{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=-i\,k\,\eta\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times Q_{m}+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{h}\right)

\displaystyle=-i\,k\,\eta\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times\hat{Q}_{m}-i\,k\,\eta\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times(Q_{m}-\hat{Q}_{m})+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{h}\right)

\displaystyle=-i\,k\,\eta\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times\hat{Q}_{m}+\mathcal{O}\left(|\eta|\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}|e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\cdot\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}|Q_{m}-\hat{Q}_{m}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(a^{h}\right)

\displaystyle=-i\,k\,\eta\,\sum_{m=1}^{\aleph}\,e^{i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\hat{x}\times\hat{Q}_{m}+\mathcal{O}(a^{\frac{h}{3}})

by taking
t\leq 1-\frac{h}{3}
for
\frac{9}{11}<h<1
, which completes the proof.
∎

5.
Proof of the a-priori estimates of Section
2
.

5.1.
Proof of Proposition
2.2
.

Recall the Lippmann-Schwinger equation in
\mathbb{L}^{2}(D)
presented in Proposition
2.1
that

E^{T}+\eta\,\nabla M^{k}(E^{T})-k^{2}\,\eta\,N^{k}(E^{T})=E^{Inc},\quad\mbox{in }D.

After scaling to
B
, we obtain

(5.1)

\tilde{E}^{T}+\eta\,\nabla M^{ka}(\tilde{E}^{T})-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,N^{ka}(\tilde{E}^{T})=\tilde{E}^{Inc},\quad\mbox{in }B.

To study
(\ref{Gc})
in
\mathbb{L}^{2}
, we project it onto each subspace by writing
\tilde{E}^{T}
over
\left(e^{(i)}_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}},i=1,2,3.

(1)

Taking the
\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)
-inner product with respect to
e^{(1)}_{n}
, then

\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle+\eta\,\langle\nabla M^{ka}(\tilde{E}^{T}),e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\langle N^{ka}(\tilde{E}^{T}),e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle=\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle.

By using the fact that
\nabla M^{ka}
is vanishing on
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\left(1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda^{(1)}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle+k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\langle\left(N^{ka}-N^{0}\right)(\tilde{E}^{T}),e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{expansion-Nk})}{=}

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle+k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\frac{i\,k\,a}{4\,\pi}\,\langle\int_{B}\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy,e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\frac{1}{4\,\pi}\,\sum_{j\geq 1}\left(ika\right)^{j+1}\langle\int_{B}\frac{\left|\cdot-y\right|^{j}}{(j+1)!}\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy,e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle.

From the mutual orthogonality of the decomposed three subspaces introduced in (
2.1
), since constant vectors are in
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
, we deduce that
\int_{B}e^{(1)}_{n}(y)\,dy=0,\;\forall\;n\,\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then,

\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle=\frac{1}{\left(1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda^{(1)}_{n}\right)}\left[\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle+\frac{k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\sum_{j\geq 1}\left(ika\right)^{j+1}\langle\int_{B}\frac{\left|\cdot-y\right|^{j}}{(j+1)!}\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy,e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\right].

Taking the modulus, there holds

\displaystyle\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left|1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda^{(1)}_{n}\right|}\left[\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\right|+a^{2}\,\left(\sum_{j\geq 1}\left|\langle\int_{B}\frac{\left|\cdot-y\right|^{j}}{(j+1)!}\,\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy,e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right],

This implies,

(5.2)

\displaystyle\left\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\frac{\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}}{\left|1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda^{(1)}_{n}\right|^{2}}+a^{4-2h}\,\sum_{n}\sum_{j\geq 1}\left|\langle\int_{B}\frac{\left|\cdot-y\right|^{j}}{(j+1)!}\,\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy,e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\frac{\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}}{\left|1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda^{(1)}_{n}\right|^{2}}+a^{4-2h}\,\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}.

(2)

Taking the
\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)
-inner product with respect to
e^{(2)}_{n}
, then

\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e_{n}^{(2)}\rangle+\eta\,\langle\nabla M^{ka}(\tilde{E}^{T}),e_{n}^{(2)}\rangle-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\langle N^{ka}(\tilde{E}^{T}),e_{n}^{(2)}\rangle=\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e_{n}^{(2)}\rangle.

Passing to the adjoint operators for both
N^{ka}
and
\nabla M^{ka}
, then

(5.3)

\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e_{n}^{(2)}\rangle+\eta\,\langle\tilde{E}^{T},\nabla M^{-ka}\left(e_{n}^{(2)}\right)\rangle-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\langle\tilde{E}^{T},N^{-ka}\left(e_{n}^{(2)}\right)\rangle=\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e_{n}^{(2)}\rangle.

Since, for
x\in B
, we have

\nabla M^{-ka}\left(e_{n}^{(2)}\right)(x):=\underset{x}{\nabla}\int_{B}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{-ka}(x,y)\cdot e_{n}^{(2)}(y)\,dy=-\underset{x}{\nabla}\underset{x}{\mathrm{div}\,}\int_{B}\Phi_{-ka}(x,y)e_{n}^{(2)}(y)\,dy=-\underset{x}{\nabla}\underset{x}{\mathrm{div}\,}N^{-ka}\left(e_{n}^{(2)}\right)(x).

Using the identity that
\Delta+Curl\circ Curl=\nabla\,\mathrm{div}\,
, we get

\displaystyle\nabla M^{-ka}\left(e_{n}^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\left(\underset{x}{\Delta}+\underset{x}{Curl}\circ\underset{x}{Curl}\right)N^{-ka}\left(e_{n}^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-ka)^{2}N^{-ka}\left(e_{n}^{(2)}\right)+e_{n}^{(2)}-\underset{x}{Curl}\left(N^{-ka}\left(Curl\,e_{n}^{(2)}\right)-SL^{-ka}\left(\nu\times e_{n}^{(2)}\right)\right),

where
SL^{-ka}
is the Single Layer operator

SL^{-ka}(\nu\times e_{n}^{(2)})(\cdot):=\int_{\partial B}\Phi_{-ka}(\cdot,y)\cdot(\nu\times e_{n}^{(2)})(y)\,d\sigma(y).

Since
Curl\left(e_{n}^{(2)}\right)=0
in the domain and
\nu\times e_{n}^{(2)}=0
on the boundary, the previous equation will be reduced to

(5.4)

\nabla M^{-ka}\left(e_{n}^{(2)}\right)=(-ka)^{2}N^{-ka}\left(e_{n}^{(2)}\right)+e_{n}^{(2)}.

By plugging this into equation
(\ref{aze})
, we obtain

\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e_{n}^{(2)}\rangle\left(1+\eta\right)=\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e_{n}^{(2)}\rangle.

Since
\tilde{E}^{Inc}\in\mathbb{H}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)=\left(\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)\oplus\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic\right)\perp\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right)
, then

\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e_{n}^{(2)}\rangle=\left(1+\eta\right)^{-1}\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e_{n}^{(2)}\rangle=0,

and

(5.5)

\left\|\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}:=\sum_{n}\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(2)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}=0,

which proves (
2.13
).

(3)

Taking the
\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)
-inner product with respect to
e^{(3)}_{n}
, then

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle+\eta\,\langle\nabla M^{ka}(\tilde{E}^{T}),e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\langle N^{ka}(\tilde{E}^{T}),e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle=\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle.

Since on this subspace, we have
\nabla M(e^{(3)}_{n})=\lambda^{(3)}_{n}e^{(3)}_{n}
, then we obtain that

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle\left(1+\eta\,\lambda^{(3)}_{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle+k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\langle N^{ka}(\tilde{E}^{T}),e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle-\eta\,\langle\left(\nabla M^{ka}-\nabla M\right)\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right),e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle

(5.6)

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\left(1+\eta\,\lambda^{(3)}_{n}\right)}\left[\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle+\eta\,\langle\left(k^{2}\,a^{2}\,N^{ka}-\nabla M^{ka}+\nabla M\right)\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right),e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle\right].

Using
(\ref{expansion-gradMk})
and
(\ref{expansion-Nk})
, we rewrite the second term on the right hand side of the previous equation as

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left(k^{2}\,a^{2}\,N^{ka}-\nabla M^{ka}+\nabla M\right)\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(ka\right)^{2}}{2}\,N\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right)+\frac{\left(ka\right)^{2}}{2}\int_{B}\Phi_{0}(\cdot,y)\frac{A(\cdot,y)\cdot\tilde{E}^{T}(y)}{\|x-y\|^{2}}dy+\frac{i\left(ka\right)^{3}}{6\pi}\int_{B}\tilde{E}^{T}(y)dy

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\;\sum_{j\geq 3}\left(ika\right)^{j+1}\;\int_{B}\;\frac{\underset{x}{Hess}\left(\left\|x-y\right\|^{j}\right)}{(j+1)!}\cdot\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy+\frac{k^{2}a^{2}}{4\pi}\;\sum_{j\geq 1}(ika)^{j+1}\;\int_{B}\;\frac{\left\|\cdot-y\right\|^{j}}{(j+1)!}\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy.

Then equation
(\ref{qdfsg})
becomes,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle}{\left(1+\eta\,\lambda^{(3)}_{n}\right)}+\frac{\eta}{\left(1+\eta\,\lambda^{(3)}_{n}\right)}\Bigg[\frac{\left(ka\right)^{2}}{2}\,\langle N\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right),e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{\left(ka\right)^{2}}{2}\,\langle\int_{B}\Phi_{0}(\cdot,y)\frac{A(\cdot,y)\cdot\tilde{E}^{T}(y)}{\|x-y\|^{2}}dy,e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle+\frac{i\left(ka\right)^{3}}{6\pi}\langle\int_{B}\tilde{E}^{T}(y)dy,e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{4\pi}\;\sum_{j\geq 3}\left(ika\right)^{j+1}\langle\int_{B}\;\frac{\underset{x}{Hess}\left(\left\|x-y\right\|^{j}\right)}{(j+1)!}\cdot\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy,e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{k^{2}a^{2}}{4\pi}\;\sum_{j\geq 1}(ika)^{j+1}\langle\int_{B}\;\frac{\left\|\cdot-y\right\|^{j}}{(j+1)!}\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy,e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle\Bigg],

and

\displaystyle\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{T},e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}}{\left|1+\eta\,\lambda^{(3)}_{n}\right|^{2}}+\Bigg[a^{4}\left|\langle N\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right),e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}+a^{4}\left|\langle\int_{B}\Phi_{0}(\cdot,y)\frac{A(\cdot,y)\cdot\tilde{E}^{T}(y)}{\|x-y\|^{2}}dy,e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle a^{6}\left|\langle\int_{B}\tilde{E}^{T}(y)dy,e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}+a^{8}\sum_{j\geq 3}\left|\langle\int_{B}\;\frac{\underset{x}{Hess}\left(\left\|\cdot-y\right\|^{j}\right)}{(j+1)!}\cdot\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy,e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+a^{8}\;\sum_{j\geq 1}\left|\langle\int_{B}\;\frac{\left\|\cdot-y\right\|^{j}}{(j+1)!}\tilde{E}^{T}(y)\,dy,e^{(3)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}\Bigg].

Taking the series with respect to
n
on the both sides and using the continuity of the Newtonian potential operator, we deduce that

(5.9)

\displaystyle\left\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle a^{4}\;\left\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{Inc}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}+\Bigg[a^{4}\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}+a^{4}\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}+a^{6}\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle a^{8}\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\sum_{j\geq 3}\int_{B}\int_{B}\frac{\left|\underset{x}{Hess}\left(\left\|x-y\right\|^{j}\right)\right|^{2}}{\left((j+1)!\right)^{2}}dydx

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle a^{8}\;\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\sum_{j\geq 1}\int_{B}\int_{B}\;\frac{\left\|x-y\right\|^{2j}}{\left((j+1)!\right)^{2}}\,dydx\Bigg].

We prove that the two series, on
j
index, are converging. For the first one, we have
3
3

3

Straightforward computations allow us to deduce that

\underset{x}{Hess}\left(\left\|x-y\right\|^{j}\right)=j\,\left\|x-y\right\|^{j-2}\,I+j\,(j-2)\,\left\|x-y\right\|^{j-4}(x-y)\otimes(x-y).

(5.10)

S_{1}:=\sum_{j\geq 3}\int_{B}\int_{B}\frac{\left|\underset{x}{Hess}\left(\left\|x-y\right\|^{j}\right)\right|^{2}}{\left((j+1)!\right)^{2}}dydx\leq\left|B\right|^{2}\,\sum_{j\geq 3}\frac{j^{2}\,\left(diam(B)^{2}\right)^{(j-2)}}{\left((j+1)!\right)^{2}}<+\infty,

and for the second one, we have

(5.11)

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\underset{j\geq 1}{\sum}\int_{B}\int_{B}\;\frac{\left\|x-y\right\|^{2j}}{\left((j+1)!\right)^{2}}\,dydx\leq|B|^{2}\,\underset{j\geq 1}{\sum}\;\frac{\left(diam(B)^{2}\right)^{j}}{(j+1)!(j+1)!}<+\infty,

where
diam(B)
is the diameter of the domain
B
.
4
4

4

Recall that
\underset{n}{\sum}a_{n}
converge if
\underset{n}{\lim}\;(a_{n+1}/a_{n})\overset{n\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}0
.
Equation
(\ref{Hsah})
becomes,

(5.12)

\left\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\lesssim a^{4}\;\left\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{Inc}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}+a^{4}\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}.

Now, by adding
(\ref{12120111})
,
(\ref{Exact2ndproj})
and
(\ref{LZ01N})
, we can obtain that for

(5.13)

h<2,

there holds the following formula

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{3}\left\|\overset{j}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{T}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)},

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\frac{\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}}{\left|1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda^{(1)}_{n}\right|^{2}}+a^{4}\;\left\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{Inc}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}

Since
\left\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{Inc}\right)\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\sim 1
, we deduce:

\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\lesssim a^{-2h}\,\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n_{0}}\rangle\right|^{2}+\sum_{n\neq n_{0}}\left|\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}+a^{4}.

To compute
\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle
, we write
e^{(1)}_{n}=Curl\left(\phi_{n}\right)
, then with integration by parts, we have

\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(1)}_{n}\rangle=\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},Curl\left(\phi_{n}\right)\rangle=\langle Curl\left(\tilde{E}^{Inc}\right),\phi_{n}\rangle=i\,k\,a\langle\tilde{H}^{Inc},\phi_{n}\rangle\sim a.

Then,

\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\lesssim a^{2-2h}\,\left|\langle\tilde{H}^{Inc},\phi_{n_{0}}\rangle\right|^{2}+a^{2}\,\sum_{n\neq n_{0}}\left|\langle\tilde{H}^{Inc},\phi_{n}\rangle\right|^{2}+a^{4}\lesssim a^{2-2h}+a^{2}+a^{4}.

Therefore we deduce that

(5.14)

\left\|\tilde{E}^{T}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)}\sim a^{1-h}.

This ends the proof of Proposition
2.2
.

5.2.
Proof of Proposition
2.3
.

Before presenting the proof of Proposition
2.3
, to write short formulas, we set the following volume integral operator:

\bm{T}_{k}:=\left(I+\eta\,\nabla M^{k}-k^{2}\,\eta\,N^{k}\right).

Since the operators
\nabla M^{-k}
and
N^{-k}
are the adjoint operators of
\nabla M^{k}
and
N^{k}
, respectively, we deduce that
\bm{T}_{-\,k}
is the adjoint operator of
\bm{T}_{k}
.
In addition, we state the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

Recall that
e_{n}^{(1)}
is the eigenfunction given by Subsection
2.3
. Then there holds

(5.15)

\bm{T}_{-\,k\,a}^{-1}\left(e_{n}^{(1)}\right)=\frac{1}{\left(1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n}^{(1)}\right)}e_{n}^{(1)}+R_{n},

where

(5.16)

R_{n}:=\frac{1\mp c_{0}a^{h}}{4\pi\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}\,\left(1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n}^{(1)}\right)}\sum_{\ell\geq 1}(-ika)^{\ell+1}\bm{T}_{-\,k\,a}^{-1}\int_{B}\;\frac{\left\|\cdot-y\right\|^{\ell}}{(\ell+1)!}e_{n}^{(1)}(y)\,dy.

Proof.

\displaystyle\bm{T}_{-\,k\,a}\left(e_{n}^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n}^{(1)}\right)e_{n}^{(1)}-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\left(N^{-ka}-N\right)\left(e_{n}^{(1)}\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{expansion-Nk})}{=}

\displaystyle\left(1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n}^{(1)}\right)e_{n}^{(1)}-\frac{k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}}{4\pi}\;\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{(-ika)^{\ell+1}}{(\ell+1)!}\;\int_{B}\;\left\|\cdot-y\right\|^{\ell}e_{n}^{(1)}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{choice-k-1st-regime})}{=}

\displaystyle\left(1-k^{2}\,\eta\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n}^{(1)}\right)e_{n}^{(1)}-\frac{1\mp c_{0}\,a^{h}}{4\pi\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}}\;\sum_{\ell\geq 1}\frac{(-ika)^{\ell+1}}{(\ell+1)!}\;\int_{B}\;\left\|\cdot-y\right\|^{\ell}e_{n}^{(1)}(y)\,dy.

The remainder of the proof consists in taking the inverse of the operator appearing on the left hand side.
∎

Now we prove Proposition
2.3
as follows.
Recall the Lippmann-Schwinger equation (

)

\displaystyle E^{T}(x)+\eta\,\sum_{j=1}^{\aleph}\underset{x}{\nabla}M^{k}(E_{j}^{T})(x)-k^{2}\,\eta\,\sum_{j=1}^{\aleph}\,N^{k}(E_{j}^{T})(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{Inc}(x),\quad x\in D=\overset{\aleph}{\underset{j=1}{\cup}}D_{j}.

By Proposition

), we can write
E_{j}^{T}=\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{j}^{T}\right)+\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{j}^{T}\right),\;j=1,\cdots,\aleph

i)

Taking the inverse operator of

\displaystyle E_{j_{0}}^{T}(x)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{j_{0}}^{T}\right)(x)

\displaystyle k^{2}\;\int_{D_{j}}\Phi_{k}(x,y)\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{j}^{T}\right)(y)\;dy.
